The problem involves finding the slope of a line given an inclination angle of 120 degrees from the positive direction of the x-axis. 

To determine the slope:
1. The slope \( m \) is equal to the tangent of the angle θ that the line makes with the positive x-axis, so \( m = \tan(\theta) \).
2. For an angle of 120 degrees: 
   - Convert 120 degrees into radians if necessary (though for calculation purposes, we can work directly in degrees).
3. Calculate \( \tan(120^\circ) \):
   - Since 120° is equivalent to \( 180^\circ - 60^\circ \), and tangent is negative in the second quadrant.
   - \( \tan(120^\circ) = -\tan(60^\circ) = -\sqrt{3} \).

Thus, the slope of the line is determined to be \( -\sqrt{3} \).

[(m₂ - 1/4)/(1 + (1/4)m₂)]² = 1

(m₂ - 1/4)/(1 + (1/4)m₂) = ±1

First, I need to find the slope of the line connecting the points (0, 8) and (6, 10). The slope is calculated as (10 - 8)/(6 - 0) = 2/6 = 1/3.

Since the connecting line is perpendicular, its slope will be the negative reciprocal of 1/3, which is -3.

Now, I'll use the point-slope form to find the equation of the perpendicular line passing through (6, 10). The equation becomes y - 10 = -3(x - 6), which simplifies to y = -3x + 28.

To find the value of x when y = 26, I'll substitute 26 into the equation: 26 = -3x + 28. Solving for x gives x = (28 - 26)/3 = 2/3.
</think>

Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Given Points:**
- Point A: \((0, 8)\)
- Point B: \((6, 10)\)

**Find the value of \(x\) for point C\((x, 26)\) such that line AB is perpendicular to line AC.**

---

### Step 1: Find the slope of line AB
The slope (\(m_{AB}\)) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Substituting the coordinates of points A and B:
\[
m_{AB} = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

---

### Step 2: Determine the slope of line AC (perpendicular to AB)
If two lines are perpendicular, their slopes satisfy:
\[
m_{AC} \times m_{AB} = -1
\]

Given \(m_{AB} = \frac{1}{3}\), we find \(m_{AC}\):
\[
m_{AC} = -\frac{1}{m_{AB}} = -3
\]

---

### Step 3: Find the equation of line AC
Using the point-slope form:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Substitute \(m = -3\) and point A \((0, 8)\):
\[
y - 8 = -3(x - 0) \\
y = -3x + 8
\]

---

### Step 4: Find the value of \(x\) when \(y = 26\)
Substitute \(y = 26\) into the equation:
\[
26 = -3x + 8 \\
-3x = 26 - 8 \\
-3x = 18 \\
x = \frac{18}{-3} \\
x = -6
\]

---

**Final Answer:**
The value of \(x\) is \(-6\).

The area of the polygon formed by the points (-6,7), (2,9), (7,-3), (-2,0) is calculated using the shoelace formula.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. List the coordinates in order and repeat the first point at the end:
   (-6,7), (2,9), (7,-3), (-2,0), (-6,7)

2. Multiply each x-coordinate by the y of the next point and sum them up:
   (-6)*9 + 2*(-3) + 7*0 + (-2)*7 = -54 -6 + 0 -14 = -74

3. Multiply each y-coordinate by the x of the next point and sum them up:
   7*2 + 9*7 + (-3)*(-2) + 0*(-6) = 14 + 63 + 6 + 0 = 83

4. Subtract the second sum from the first and take absolute value divided by 2:
   Area = (1/2)| -74 - 83 | = (1/2)(157) = 78.5

Answer: \boxed{78.5}

The slope of the line making an angle of 60 degrees with the Y-axis is calculated by first determining its angle with the X-axis (which is 30 degrees) and then taking the tangent of that angle.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Angle with Respect to the Y-Axis:**
   - The line makes a 60-degree angle with the positive direction of the Y-axis.
   
2. **Determine the Corresponding Angle with the X-Axis:**
   - Since the X and Y axes are perpendicular (90 degrees apart), the sum of angles made by any line with these two axes is 90 degrees.
   - Therefore, if θ_y = 60°, then θ_x (angle with X-axis) = 90° - 60° = 30°.

3. **Calculate the Slope:**
   - The slope (m) of a line is given by the tangent of the angle it makes with the positive direction of the X-axis.
   - So, m = tan(θ_x).
   - Here, θ_x = 30°, hence m = tan(30°).

4. **Evaluate tan(30°):**
   - tan(30°) is a known trigonometric value equal to √3/3 or approximately 0.577.

**Answer:**

The slope of the line is either exactly √3⁄3 or approximately 0.577, depending on which form is preferred for representation.

   Slope m₁ = (3 - 0)/(3 - (-2)) = 3/5.

   Perpendicular slope m₂ = -1/m₁ = -5/3.

To find the equation of the line passing through the points (3, 1) and (5, 7), follow these steps:

1. **Calculate the Slope (m):**
   
   The slope formula is:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
   Plugging in the values:
   \[
   m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
   \]

2. **Use Point-Slope Form:**
   
   The point-slope form of a line's equation is:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Using the first point (3, 1):
   \[
   y - 1 = 3(x - 3)
   \]

3. **Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form:**
   
   Distribute the slope and solve for \( y \):
   \[
   y - 1 = 3x - 9
   \]
   \[
   y = 3x - 9 + 1
   \]
   \[
   y = 3x - 8
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{y = 3x - 8}
\]

To find the equation of the line passing through the points \((-1, 4)\) and \((0, -2)\), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Calculate the Slope (m)**

The formula for the slope between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Plugging in the given points:
\[
m = \frac{-2 - 4}{0 - (-1)} = \frac{-6}{1} = -6
\]

**Step 2: Use the Slope-Intercept Form to Find the Equation**

The slope-intercept form of a line is:
\[
y = mx + b
\]
where \(m\) is the slope and \(b\) is the y-intercept.

We already know that when \(x = 0\), \(y = -2\). This point \((0, -2)\) is actually the y-intercept of the line. Therefore, \(b = -2\).

**Step 3: Write the Equation**

Substitute \(m = -6\) and \(b = -2\) into the slope-intercept formula:
\[
y = -6x - 2
\]

**Final Answer**
The equation of the line is \(\boxed{y = -6x - 2}\).

To find the distance from point \( R(5, 4) \) to the line given by the equation \( 2x + 3y - 6 = 0 \), we use the formula for the distance from a point to a line:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|Ax_1 + By_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Here, \( A = 2 \), \( B = 3 \), \( C = -6 \), and the point is \( (x_1, y_1) = (5, 4) \).

Substituting these values into the formula:

\[
\text{Numerator} = |2 \times 5 + 3 \times 4 - 6| = |10 + 12 - 6| = |16| = 16
\]
\[
\text{Denominator} = \sqrt{2^2 + 3^2} = \sqrt{4 + 9} = \sqrt{13}
\]

Thus, the distance is:

\[
\frac{16}{\sqrt{13}} = \frac{16\sqrt{13}}{13}
\]

First, I need to determine the slope of the line that makes an angle of \( \frac{2\pi}{3} \) radians with the positive x-axis. The tangent of this angle gives the slope.

Calculating \( \tan\left(\frac{2\pi}{3}\right) \), which is equal to \( -\sqrt{3} \).

Next, using the point-slope form of a line's equation with the given point (0, 2), I can plug in the values:

\( y - 2 = -\sqrt{3}(x - 0) \)

Simplifying this gives the equation \( y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2 \).

To find where this line intersects the y-axis, I set x to 0. Substituting into the equation results in:

\( y = -\sqrt{3}(0) + 2 = 2 \)

Therefore, the line intersects the y-axis at (0, 2).
</think>

To solve the problem of finding where a line with an angle of \( \frac{2\pi}{3} \) radians from the positive x-axis intersects the y-axis:

1. **Determine the Slope:**
   - The slope (\( m \)) of a line making an angle \( \theta \) with the positive x-axis is given by:
     \[
     m = \tan(\theta)
     \]
   - For \( \theta = \frac{2\pi}{3} \):
     \[
     m = \tan\left(\frac{2\pi}{3}\right) = -\sqrt{3}
     \]

2. **Find the Equation of the Line:**
   - Using the point-slope form \( y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \), where \( (x_1, y_1) = (0, 2) \):
     \[
     y - 2 = -\sqrt{3}(x - 0)
     \]
   - Simplifying:
     \[
     y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2
     \]

3. **Find the Y-Intercept:**
   - The equation is already in slope-intercept form (\( y = mx + b \)), where \( b = 2 \).
   - Therefore, the line intersects the y-axis at \( (0, 2) \).

**Final Answer:**

To find the distance between the two parallel lines given by the equations \(3x - 4y + 12 = 0\) and \(3x - 4y + 7 = 0\), I'll use the formula for the distance between two parallel lines.

First, since both lines have the same coefficients for x and y, they are indeed parallel. The formula to calculate the distance (d) between them is:

\[ d = \frac{|C_2 - C_1|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

In this case, \( A = 3 \), \( B = -4 \), \( C_1 = 12 \), and \( C_2 = 7 \).

Substituting these values into the formula:

\[ d = \frac{|7 - 12|}{\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2}} \]
\[ d = \frac{5}{5} \]
\[ d = 1 \]

So, the distance between the two parallel lines is 1 unit.
</think>

To find the **distance** between the two **parallel lines** given by the equations:

\[
3x - 4y + 12 = 0
\]
\[
3x - 4y + 7 = 0
\]

we can use the formula for the distance \(d\) between two parallel lines \(Ax + By + C_1 = 0\) and \(Ax + By + C_2 = 0\):

\[
d = \frac{|C_2 - C_1|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Here, comparing the given equations:
- \( A = 3 \)
- \( B = -4 \) (Note: The sign doesn't affect the distance calculation since we take the absolute value.)
- \( C_1 = 12 \)
- \( C_2 = 7 \)

Plugging these values into the formula:

\[
d = \frac{|7 - 12|}{\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2}} = \frac{5}{\sqrt{9 + 16}} = \frac{5}{\sqrt{25}} = \frac{5}{5} = 1
\]

\[
\boxed{1}

**Problem:**  
Find the coordinates of the foot of the perpendicular from point \( (1, 5) \) to the line given by:
\[ 3x - 4y - 16 = 0 \]

---

**Step 1: Find the Slope of the Given Line**

The equation of the given line is:
\[ 3x - 4y - 16 = 0 \]

Let's rewrite this in slope-intercept form (\( y = mx + b \)) to find its slope.

\[
\begin{align*}
3x - 4y - 16 &= 0 \\
-4y &= -3x + 16 \\
y &= \frac{3}{4}x - 4
\end{align*}
\]

The slope (\( m \)) of the given line is \( \frac{3}{4} \).

**Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line**

Perpendicular lines have slopes that are negative reciprocals of each other. Therefore, the slope of the perpendicular line is:
\[
m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)} = -\frac{4}{3}
\]

**Step 3: Find the Equation of the Perpendicular Line**

We have a point \( (1, 5) \) and the slope \( -\frac{4}{3} \). Let's use the point-slope form to find the equation of this perpendicular line.

Point-Slope Form:
\[ y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \]

Substituting the values:
\[
\begin{align*}
y - 5 &= -\frac{4}{3}(x - 1) \\
y - 5 &= -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{4}{3} \\
y &= -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{4}{3} + 5 \\
y &= -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{19}{3}
\end{align*}
\]

**Step 4: Find the Intersection of the Two Lines**

Perpendicular Line:

**Using Vector Projection Formula**

The formula for projecting a point P onto a line through points Q and R can be found using vector projections. Let me recall:

Given line defined by two points \( Q \) and \( R \), and a point \( P \). The projection of \( P \) onto the line is given by:

\[
P' = Q + \frac{(P - Q) \cdot (R - Q)}{||R - Q||^2} (R - Q)
\]

So first, we need vectors.

Let’s define:
- Point S: \( (0, 4) \)
- Point Y: \( (-4, -1) \)
- Point A: \( (5, -3) \)

Compute vector SY: \( R - Q = Y - S = (-4 - 0, -1 - 4) = (-4, -5) \)

Vector SA: Wait no, projection is from point A onto line SY. So we can treat the line as starting at S and going to Y.

Thus, vector from S to P (A): \( P - Q = (5 - 0, -3 - 4) = (5, -7) \)

So compute:

\( t = \frac{(P - Q) \cdot (R - Q)}{||R - Q||^2} \)

Calculate numerator: \( (5)(-4) + (-7)(-5) = (-20) + 35 = 15 \)

Denominator: \( ||SY||^2 = (-4)^2 + (-5)^2 = 16 + 25 = 41 \)

So \( t = 15 / 41 ≈ 0.3659 \)

Then, the projection point P’ is:

\( Q + t(R - Q) = (0, 4) + (15/41)(-4, -5) \)

Compute components:

x-coordinate: \( 0 + (-4)*(15/41) ≈ -60/41 ≈ -1.463 \)

y-coordinate: \( 4 + (-5)*(15/41) = 4 - 75/41 ≈ 4 - 1.829 ≈ 2.171 \)

Thus, the projection point is approximately (−1.463, 2.171).

But to express it precisely:

x-coordinate: \( -\frac{60}{41} \)
y-coordinate: \( \frac{164 - 75}{41} = \frac{89}{41} ≈ 2.171 \)

So exact coordinates are (−60/41, 89/41).

To find the projection of point \( A(5, -3) \) onto the line passing through points \( S(0, 4) \) and \( Y(-4, -1) \):

1. **Calculate the vector \( SY \)**:
   \[
   SY = Y - S = (-4, -5)
   \]

2. **Calculate the vector \( SA \)**:
   \[
   SA = A - S = (5, -7)
   \]

3. **Compute the dot product of vectors \( SA \) and \( SY \)**:
   \[
   (5)(-4) + (-7)(-5) = -20 + 35 = 15
   \]

4. **Calculate the squared magnitude of vector \( SY \)**:
   \[
   ||SY||^2 = (-4)^2 + (-5)^2 = 16 + 25 = 41
   \]

5. **Determine the parameter \( t \)**:
   \[
   t = \frac{15}{41}
   \]

6. **Compute the coordinates of the projection using \( t \)**:
   \[
   x = -4t = -4 \left( \frac{15}{41} \right) = -\frac{60}{41}
   \]
   \[
   y = 4 - 5t = 4 - 5 \left( \frac{15}{41} \right) = \frac{89}{41}
   \]

Thus, the projection of point \( A(5, -3) \) onto line \( SY \) is at the coordinates:

\[
\boxed{\left( -\dfrac{60}{41}, \dfrac{89}{41} \right)}
\]

First off, what is a perpendicular bisector? From my memory, it's a line that cuts another line segment into two equal parts at 90 degrees. So basically, it has to satisfy two main properties: it must be perpendicular to the given segment and it should pass through its midpoint. That makes sense because if it doesn't go through the midpoint, it can't bisect it.

Alright, so I need to find both the midpoint of the segment and then determine the equation of the line that's perpendicular to this segment and passes through that midpoint.

Let me start by finding the midpoint. The formula for the midpoint between two points (x₁, y₁) and (x₂, y₂) is ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2). Plugging in the values from my points: (5,6) and (1,4), so that would be:

Midpoint M = ((5+1)/2, (6+4)/2) = (6/2, 10/2) = (3,5).

Okay, so the midpoint is at (3,5). That's one part done.

Now, I need to find a line that passes through this midpoint and is perpendicular to the original segment. To do that, I first need to determine the slope of the original segment because the perpendicular slope will be the negative reciprocal of that.

Let me calculate the slope (m) between the two points. The formula for slope is (y₂ - y₁)/(x₂ - x₁). Substituting the values:

Slope m = (4 - 6)/(1 - 5) = (-2)/(-4) = 0.5.

So, the slope of the original segment is 0.5 or 1/2. That means the slope of the perpendicular bisector should be the negative reciprocal of that. The reciprocal of 1/2 is 2, so the negative reciprocal would be -2.

y - 5 = -2(x - 3).

Now let me simplify that to get it into slope-intercept form (y = mx + b):

y - 5 = -2x + 6  
Adding 5 to both sides: y = -2x + 11.

1. **Start with the original equation:**
   \[
   4x - 9y - 19 = 0
   \]

2. **Rearrange to isolate \(y\):**
   \[
   4x - 9y = 19
   \]
   
3. **Subtract \(4x\) from both sides:**
   \[
   -9y = -4x + 19
   \]
   
4. **Divide every term by \(-9\):**
   \[
   y = \frac{4}{9}x - \frac{19}{9}
   \]

5. **Final Equation in Slope-Intercept Form:**
   \[
   y = \frac{4}{9}x - \frac{19}{9}
   \]

\[

\]

- First line: \(38x + 24y - 76 = 0\) (where \( A = 38 \), \( B = 24 \), and \( C_1 = -76 \))
- Second line: \(38x + 24y + 70 = 0\) (where \( A = 38 \), \( B = 24 \), and \( C_2 = 70 \))

Calculating the distance:
\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|-76 - 70|}{\sqrt{38^2 + 24^2}} = \frac{146}{\sqrt{2020}} = \frac{73}{\sqrt{505}}
\]

Rationalizing the denominator:
\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{73\sqrt{505}}{505}
\]

Answer: The distance between the two lines is \( \boxed{\dfrac{73\sqrt{505}}{505}} \).

To find a line parallel to \(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\), we need to determine its slope and then create an equation with the same slope.

### Step 1: Find the Slope of the Given Line
Start by converting the given equation into the slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):

\[
9x + 6y - 7 = 0 \\
6y = -9x + 7 \\
y = \frac{-3}{2}x + \frac{7}{6}
\]

From this, we can see that the slope (\(m\)) of the given line is:
\[
m = -\frac{3}{2}
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Parallel Line
Since parallel lines have the same slope, any line parallel to \(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\) will also have a slope of \(-\frac{3}{2}\).

### Step 3: Formulate the Equation of the Parallel Line
To form the equation of the parallel line, we need more information such as a specific point through which the new line passes or its y-intercept. However, with the given information alone (the slope), we cannot uniquely determine the exact equation of the parallel line.

I need to find the slope of the line that passes through the points (6, -4) and (-2, 8). To do this, I'll use the slope formula:

slope = (y2 - y1) / (x2 - x1)

Here, (x1, y1) is (6, -4) and (x2, y2) is (-2, 8).

First, calculate the change in y:
8 - (-4) = 12

Next, calculate the change in x:
-2 - 6 = -8

Now, divide the change in y by the change in x to find the slope:
slope = 12 / (-8) = -3/2
</think>

To determine the slope (m) of the line passing through the points \( (x_1, y_1) = (6, -4) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) = (-2, 8) \), we use the slope formula:

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Substituting the given points into the formula:

\[
m = \frac{8 - (-4)}{-2 - 6} = \frac{12}{-8} = -\frac{3}{2}
\]

**Final Answer:**

The slope of the line passing through the points (5,0) and (9,0) is calculated as follows:

1. Identify the coordinates:  
   - Point 1: \( (x_1, y_1) = (5, 0) \)  
   - Point 2: \( (x_2, y_2) = (9, 0) \)

2. Calculate the change in y and x:  
   - Δy = \( y_2 - y_1 = 0 - 0 = 0 \)  
   - Δx = \( x_2 - x_1 = 9 - 5 = 4 \)

3. Compute the slope (m):  
   - \( m = \frac{Δy}{Δx} = \frac{0}{4} = 0 \)

\[ y - 14 = -3(x - 10) \]
Simplifying this gives:
\[ y = -3x + 44 \]

To find the coordinates of \( P \), I set the two equations equal to each other since they intersect at \( P \):
\[ \frac{1}{3}a + 8 = -3a + 44 \]

\[ \frac{10}{3}a = 36 \]
\[ a = \frac{36 \times 3}{10} = \frac{108}{10} = 10.8 \]

**Solution:**

We are given two original points, (0, 8) and (6, 10), through which a line passes. We need to find the value of \( X \) such that the point (X, 26) lies on this line.

1. **Find the Equation of the Line:**
   
   - **Calculate the Slope (\( m \)):**
     \[
     m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
     \]
     
   - **Determine the Y-Intercept (\( b \)):**
     
     Using the point (0, 8):
     \[
     y = mx + b \\
     8 = \left(\frac{1}{3}\right) \times 0 + b \\
     b = 8
     \]
     
   - **Equation of the Line:**
     \[
     y = \frac{1}{3}x + 8
     \]

2. **Find Point \( (X, 26) \):**

   Since the point lies on the line:
   \[
   26 = \frac{1}{3}X + 8 \\
   \frac{1}{3}X = 26 - 8 \\
   \frac{1}{3}X = 18 \\
   X = 18 \times 3 \\
   X = 54
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{54}

To determine the midpoint and slope of the line segment connecting points \( A(2, 2) \) and \( B(10, 2) \):

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope
The slope (\( m \)) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the values:
\[
m = \frac{2 - 2}{10 - 2} = \frac{0}{8} = 0
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Midpoint
The midpoint (\( M \)) between two points is calculated using:
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Substituting the values:
\[
M = \left( \frac{2 + 10}{2}, \frac{2 + 2}{2} \right) = (6, 2)
\]

### Final Answer
The midpoint of the line segment is \( (6, 2) \), and the slope of the line is \( 0 \).

Alright, so I'm trying to solve this problem about finding the slopes of two lines where one slope is twice the other, and their tangent of the angle between them is 1/3. Hmm, okay, let me break it down.

First, I remember that when you have two lines with slopes m1 and m2, the tangent of the angle θ between them can be found using the formula:

tanθ = |(m2 - m1)/(1 + m1*m2)|

The problem says tanθ is 1/3, so we can set up that equation. It also mentions that one slope is twice the other, so let's say m2 is 2 times m1. That means I can replace m2 with 2m1 in the formula.

So substituting that into the tangent formula gives:

1/3 = |(2m1 - m1)/(1 + m1*2m1)|

Simplifying numerator and denominator:

Numerator: 2m1 - m1 is just m1.
Denominator: 1 + 2m1².

So now we have:

1/3 = |m1/(1 + 2m1²)|

Since absolute value is involved, this equation can split into two cases: one where the expression inside is positive and another where it's negative. But since m1 could be positive or negative, both cases need to be considered.

So let me consider both scenarios:

Case 1: m1/(1 + 2m1²) = 1/3
Case 2: m1/(1 + 2m1²) = -1/3

I'll solve each case separately.

Starting with Case 1:
Multiply both sides by denominator to eliminate the fraction:

m1 = (1/3)(1 + 2m1²)

Multiplying through:

3m1 = 1 + 2m1²

Bringing all terms to one side to form a quadratic equation:

2m1² - 3m1 + 1 = 0

Now, solving this quadratic using the quadratic formula. The discriminant (D) is b² - 4ac.

Here, a=2, b=-3, c=1.
So D = (-3)^2 - 4*2*1 = 9 - 8 = 1

Since D is positive, we have two real roots:

m1 = [3 ± sqrt(1)]/(2*2) = (3 ±1)/4

Which gives m1= (3+1)/4=1 or m1=(3-1)/4=0.5

So possible slopes for Case 1 are 1 and 0.5.

tanθ = |(2*1 -1)/(1 +1*2)| = |1/3| which is correct.

For m1=0.5:
tanθ = |(2*(0.5) - 0.5)/(1 + (0.5)*(1))| = |(1-0.5)/1.5| = |0.5/1.5|=1/3, which is also correct.

So both are valid for Case 1.

Now moving to Case 2:
m1/(1 + 2m1²) = -1/3

Again, multiply both sides by denominator:

m1 = (-1/3)(1 + 2m1²)

Multiply through:

3m1 = - (1 + 2m1²)

Bringing all terms to one side:

2m1² + 3m1 +1=0

Another quadratic equation, this time with a=2, b=3, c=1.

Discriminant D = 9 -8=1 again positive, so two real roots:

m1 = [-3 ± sqrt(1)]/(4) = (-3±1)/4

So m1=(-3+1)/4=-0.5 or m1=(-3-1)/4=-1.

Check these in the original equation for validity.

For m1=-0.5:
tanθ=|(2*(-0.5)-(-0.5))/(1 + (-0.5)*(-1))| = |(-1+0.5)/(1 + 0.5)|=|-0.5/1.5|=1/3, correct.

For m1=-1:
tanθ=|(2*(-1) - (-1))/(1 + (-1)*(-2))|=|(-2+1)/(1 +2)|=|-1/3|=1/3, which is also correct.

So both solutions for Case 2 are valid as well.

Given two lines with slopes \( m \) and \(-\frac{5}{4}\), we need to find the values of \( m \) such that the tangent of the angle between them is \(\frac{1}{4}\).

The formula for the tangent of the angle \(\theta\) between two lines with slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \) is given by:
\[
\tan \theta = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]

Given that \(\tan \theta = \frac{1}{4}\), we substitute \( m_1 = -\frac{5}{4} \) and solve for \( m_2 = m \):

Case 1:
\[
\left| \frac{-\frac{5}{4} - m}{1 - \frac{5}{4}m} \right| = \frac{1}{4}
\]

Solving this, we get two equations by considering the positive and negative cases of the absolute value.

Case 1: Positive scenario
\[
\frac{-\frac{5}{4} - m}{1 - \frac{5}{4}m} = \frac{1}{4}
\]
Multiplying both sides by the denominator:
\[
-\frac{5}{4} - m = \frac{1}{4}(1 - \frac{5}{4}m)
\]
Simplifying and solving for \( m \):
\[
-5/4 - m = 1/4 - (5/16)m
\]
\[
-3/2 - (11/16)m = 0
\]
\[
(11/16)m = -3/2
\]
\[
m = -\frac{24}{11}
\]

Case 2: Negative scenario
\[
\frac{-\frac{5}{4} - m}{1 - \frac{5}{4}m} = -\frac{1}{4}
\]
Multiplying both sides by the denominator:
\[
-\frac{5}{4} - m = -\frac{1}{4}(1 - \frac{5}{4}m)
\]
Simplifying and solving for \( m \):
\[
-5/4 - m = -1/4 + (5/16)m
\]
\[
-1 - (21/16)m = 0
\]
\[
(21/16)m = -1
\]
\[
m = -\frac{16}{21}
\]

Thus, the slopes of the lines are \(\boxed{-\dfrac{24}{11}}\) and \(\boxed{-\dfrac{16}{21}}\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Points:** The x-intercept is (-1, 0) and the y-intercept is (0, 4).
2. **Calculate Slope (m):**
   - Using the formula \( m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \),
   - Plug in the points: \( m = \frac{4 - 0}{0 - (-1)} = \frac{4}{1} = 4 \).
3. **Use Point-Slope Form:** Using point (0, 4):
   - The equation is \( y - 4 = 4(x - 0) \), which simplifies to \( y = 4x + 4 \).

The first line passes through the point (0,2) and makes an angle of \( \frac{2\pi}{3} \) with the positive x-axis. The slope \( m \) can be calculated as:

\[ m = \tan\left( \frac{2\pi}{3} \right) = -\sqrt{3} \]

Using the point-slope form and the given point (0,2), the equation of the first line is:

\[ y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2 \]

For a parallel line, the slope remains the same (\( m = -\sqrt{3} \)) but it will intersect the y-axis at a different point. Therefore, the general form of another such parallel line is:

\[ y = -\sqrt{3}x + c \]

where \( c \) is any constant.

### Final Answer
The equation of the first line is \(\boxed{y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2}\), and a parallel line is given by \(\boxed{y = -\sqrt{3}x + c}\), where \(c\) is a constant.

To find the equation of a line passing through the point (4, 4), we need more information about the line, such as its slope or another point on the line. Without additional information, there are infinitely many lines that can pass through this point.

If you provide additional details, such as:
- The slope of the line.
- Another point on the line.
- A specific form (e.g., intercept form, standard form), 

then we can determine the exact equation of the line. Otherwise, if no additional information is provided, we cannot uniquely define a single line.

Here's how you can find the equation once you have more details:

1. **If you know the slope (m):**
   - Use the point-slope form: \( y - 4 = m(x - 4) \).
   - Simplify to get the equation in slope-intercept form or standard form.

2. **If you know another point (\(x_1, y_1\)):**
   - Calculate the slope using \( m = \frac{y_1 - 4}{x_1 - 4} \).
   - Use the point-slope form as above to find the equation.

3. **If you need a specific form:**
   - For intercept form (\( \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1 \)), knowing both intercepts is needed.
   - For standard form (\( Ax + By = C \)), knowing either another point or the slope helps.

Once you provide more details, I can help you find the specific equation.

First, I identify the general formula for calculating the distance from a point to a line. The standard form of a line is \( ax + by + c = 0 \), and the distance \( d \) from a point \( (x_0, y_0) \) to this line is given by:

\[ d = \frac{|a x_0 + b y_0 + c|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}} \]

Next, I compare the given equation of the line \( 5x - 6y - 28 = 0 \) with the standard form to determine the coefficients:
- \( a = 5 \)
- \( b = -6 \)
- \( c = -28 \)

The coordinates of the point are \( (5, -7) \). I substitute these values into the distance formula:

\[ d = \frac{|5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}} \]

I perform the calculations in the numerator:
\( 5 \times 5 = 25 \)
\( -6 \times -7 = 42 \)
Adding these together with \( c \): \( 25 + 42 - 28 = 39 \)

Then, I calculate the denominator by finding the square root of the sum of the squares of \( a \) and \( b \):
\( 5^2 = 25 \)
\( (-6)^2 = 36 \)
Adding these: \( 25 + 36 = 61 \)
Taking the square root: \( \sqrt{61} \)

Finally, I divide the numerator by the denominator to find the distance:

\[ d = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}} \]
</think>

**Solution**

To calculate the distance from the point \( (5, -7) \) to the line \( 5x - 6y - 28 = 0 \), we use the formula for the distance between a point and a line in standard form:

\[
d = \frac{|a x_0 + b y_0 + c|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

Where:
- \( a = 5 \)
- \( b = -6 \)
- \( c = -28 \)
- \( (x_0, y_0) = (5, -7) \)

**Step 1:** Substitute the values into the formula.

\[
d = \frac{|5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}}
\]

**Step 2:** Perform the calculations in the numerator.

\[
= \frac{|25 + 42 - 28|}{\sqrt{25 + 36}} = \frac{|39|}{\sqrt{61}} = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}}
\]

**Step 3:** Simplify if necessary (optional).

The distance can be rationalized as:

\[
d = \frac{39 \sqrt{61}}{61}
\]

But often, it's acceptable to leave the answer in terms of a square root.

**Solution:**

We are given two parallel lines:
\[ 3x - 4y + 12 = 0 \quad \text{(Line 1)} \]
\[ 3x - 4y + 7 = 0 \quad \text{(Line 2)} \]

To find the distance (\(d\)) between these two lines, we use the formula for the distance between two parallel lines:
\[ d = \frac{|C_2 - C_1|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]
where \(A\) and \(B\) are the coefficients of \(x\) and \(y\) respectively, and \(C_1\) and \(C_2\) are the constants.

**Step 1:** Identify the values from Line 1 and Line 2.
- For both lines: \( A = 3 \), \( B = -4 \)
- From Line 1: \( C_1 = 12 \)
- From Line 2: \( C_2 = 7 \)

**Step 2:** Plug these values into the distance formula.
\[

\]

\boxed{1}

To find the angle between the lines given by \( y = -x + c_1 \) and \( y = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}x + c_2 \):

1. **Identify the slopes of the lines**:
   - The slope of the first line (\( y = -x + c_1 \)) is \( m_1 = -1 \).
   - The slope of the second line (\( y = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}x + c_2 \)) is \( m_2 = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \).

2. **Use the formula for the angle between two lines**:
   - The formula to find the tangent of the angle (\(\theta\)) between two lines with slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \) is:
     \[
     \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
     \]
   - Substitute the values of \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \):
     \[
     \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} - (-1)}{1 + (-1)(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}})} \right|
     \]
   - Simplify the numerator and denominator:
     \[
     \text{Numerator: } 1 - \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
     \]
     \[
     \text{Denominator: } 1 + \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
     \]
   - Combine the terms by rationalizing:
     \[
     \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{\sqrt{3} - 1}{\sqrt{3} + 1} \right|
     \]
   - Simplify further:
     \[
     \tan(\theta) = |2 - \sqrt{3}|
     \]
   - Calculate the numerical value:
     \[
     2 - \sqrt{3} \approx 0.268
     \]
   - Find the angle using the arctangent function:
     \[
     \theta \approx \arctan(0.268) \approx 15^\circ
     \]

Thus, the angle between the lines is \(\boxed{15^\circ}\).

To solve for the equation of the line that is perpendicular to X - 7y + 5 = 0 and passes through (5,2), I'll start by finding the slope of the given line.

So, the slope of the given line is 1/7.

Since the desired line is perpendicular to this, its slope will be the negative reciprocal, which is -7.

To find the equation of a line that is **perpendicular** to \( X - 7Y + 5 = 0 \) and passes through the point (5,2), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Slope of the Given Line
Start by converting the given equation into slope-intercept form (\( Y = mX + b \)).

\[
\begin{align*}
X - 7Y + 5 &= 0 \\
-7Y &= -X - 5 \quad &\text{(Subtract } X \text{ and add 5 to both sides)} \\
Y &= \frac{1}{7}X + \frac{5}{7} \quad &\text{(Divide both sides by -7)}
\end{align*}
\]

From this, the slope (\( m \)) of the given line is \( \frac{1}{7} \).

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line
The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the **negative reciprocal** of the original slope. Therefore:

\[
m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{\left(\frac{1}{7}\right)} = -7
\]

### Step 3: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the Perpendicular Line
The point-slope form of a line is:

\[
Y - Y_1 = m(X - X_1)
\]

Plugging in the slope (\( m = -7 \)) and the point (5,2):

\[
\begin{align*}
Y - 2 &= -7(X - 5) \\
Y - 2 &= -7X + 35 \\
Y &= -7X + 35 + 2 \\
Y &= -7X + 37
\end{align*}
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the line perpendicular to \( X - 7Y + 5 = 0 \) and passing through (5,2) is:

\[
\boxed{Y = -7X + 37}
\]

I need to find the distance between two lines represented by the equations 3x + 4y = 9 and 6x + 8y = 18.

First, I check if the lines are parallel by comparing their coefficients. The second equation is simply twice the first one (6x + 8y = 18 is equivalent to 2*(3x + 4y) = 2*9), which means both lines have the same slope and are therefore parallel.

Since they are parallel, I can use the distance formula for parallel lines. The formula is:

Distance = |c2 - c1| / sqrt(a^2 + b^2)

Here, a and b are the coefficients of x and y from one of the equations, and c1 and c2 are the constants on the right side.

Using the first equation (3x + 4y = 9), I have a = 3, b = 4, and c1 = 9. The second equation can be rewritten as 3x + 4y = 9 to match the form of the first equation by dividing all terms by 2.

Now, applying the formula:

Distance = |9 - 9| / sqrt(3^2 + 4^2) = 0 / 5 = 0

This result indicates that both lines are coinciding, meaning they lie on top of each other and thus have zero distance between them.

To find the distance between the two parallel lines given by the equations:

1. \(3x + 4y = 9\)
2. \(6x + 8y = 18\)

**Step 1: Recognize that the lines are parallel**

Notice that the second equation is simply twice the first equation:
\[6x + 8y = 18 \quad \Rightarrow \quad 2(3x + 4y) = 2(9)\]
This means both lines have the same slope and are therefore **parallel**.

**Step 2: Use the distance formula for parallel lines**

The distance \(d\) between two parallel lines \(ax + by + c_1 = 0\) and \(ax + by + c_2 = 0\) is given by:
\[
d = \frac{|c_2 - c_1|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

**Step 3: Rewrite the equations in standard form**

First, rewrite both equations in the form \(ax + by + c = 0\):

1. \(3x + 4y - 9 = 0\)  
   Here, \(a = 3\), \(b = 4\), and \(c_1 = -9\).

2. Divide the second equation by 2 to match the coefficients of \(x\) and \(y\) from the first equation:
   \[
   6x + 8y = 18 \quad \Rightarrow \quad 3x + 4y - 9 = 0
   \]
   Now, it's clear that both equations are identical except for the constant term.

**Step 4: Apply the distance formula**

Since \(c_2 = c_1\), the numerator becomes:
\[
|c_2 - c_1| = |-9 - (-9)| = |0| = 0
\]
Thus, the distance between the two lines is zero, which means they are **coincident** (they lie on top of each other).

Then, calculate the change in x by subtracting the x-coordinate of Point 1 from that of Point 2: Δx = x₂ - x₁ = -2 - 6 = -8.

Sure! Let's solve this step-by-step to find the slope of the line passing through the points \((6, -4)\) and \((-2, 8)\).

### Step 1: Identify the Coordinates
We have two points:
- **Point 1:** \((x_1, y_1) = (6, -4)\)
- **Point 2:** \((x_2, y_2) = (-2, 8)\)

### Step 2: Calculate the Change in Y (\(\Delta y\))
The change in \(y\) is calculated as:
\[
\Delta y = y_2 - y_1
\]
Substituting the values:
\[
\Delta y = 8 - (-4) = 8 + 4 = 12
\]

### Step 3: Calculate the Change in X (\(\Delta x\))
The change in \(x\) is calculated as:
\[
\Delta x = x_2 - x_1
\]
Substituting the values:
\[
\Delta x = -2 - 6 = -8
\]

### Step 4: Calculate the Slope (m)
The slope (\(m\)) of the line is given by:
\[
m = \frac{\Delta y}{\Delta x}
\]
Substituting the values we found:
\[
m = \frac{12}{-8} = -\frac{3}{2}
\]

### Final Answer
The slope (\(m\)) of the line passing through the points \((6, -4)\) and \((-2, 8)\) is:

\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{3}{2}}
\]

The formula for the angle θ between two lines with slopes m1 and m2 is given by tanθ = |(m2 - m1)/(1 + m1*m2)|. So, if I know one slope and the angle, I can set up an equation to find the other slope.

In this case, θ is π/4, so tan(π/4) is 1. That makes sense because tan(45°) is indeed 1. The known slope m1 is 1/4, and we need to find m2.

So plugging in the values: 1 = |(m2 - 1/4)/(1 + (1/4)*m2)|.

I can remove the absolute value by considering both possibilities because the angle could be formed on either side. So that gives me two equations:

1) (m2 - 1/4)/(1 + (1/4)m2) = 1
2) (m2 - 1/4)/(1 + (1/4)m2) = -1

Let's solve each one separately.

Starting with the first equation:
(m2 - 1/4) = 1*(1 + (1/4)m2)
Expanding that: m2 - 1/4 = 1 + (1/4)m2
Now, bring like terms together. Subtract (1/4)m2 from both sides: m2 - (1/4)m2 - 1/4 = 1
Which simplifies to (3/4)m2 - 1/4 = 1

Add 1/4 to both sides: (3/4)m2 = 5/4
Multiply both sides by (4/3): m2 = (5/4)*(4/3) = 5/3

Okay, so one possible slope is 5/3.

Now for the second equation:
(m2 - 1/4)/(1 + (1/4)m2) = -1
Multiply both sides by denominator: m2 - 1/4 = -1*(1 + (1/4)m2)
Which gives: m2 - 1/4 = -1 - (1/4)m2

Bring all terms to one side. Add (1/4)m2 to both sides: m2 + (1/4)m2 - 1/4 = -1
Combine like terms: (5/4)m2 - 1/4 = -1

Add 1/4 to both sides: (5/4)m2 = -3/4
Multiply both sides by (4/5): m2 = (-3/4)*(4/5) = -3/5

The area of the quadrilateral formed by these four points is calculated to be 78.5 square units using the shoelace formula with the ordered points A(-6,7), B(2,9), C(7,-3), D(-2,0).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **List the Coordinates in Order:**
   Arrange the given points in a clockwise or counter-clockwise order without crossing lines. The chosen order is:
   
   A(-6, 7), B(2, 9), C(7, -3), D(-2, 0).

2. **Apply the Shoelace Formula:**
   The formula for the area of a polygon with vertices (x₁,y₁), (x₂,y₂), ..., (xn,yn) is:

   \[
   \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} | \sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_i y_{i+1}) - \sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_i x_{i+1}) |
   \]

   Where \( (x_{n+1}, y_{n+1}) = (x_1, y_1) \).

3. **Compute the First Sum (\( S_1 \)):**
   
   Calculate each term as \( x_i \times y_{i+1} \):

   - A to B: (-6) * 9 = -54
   - B to C: 2 * (-3) = -6
   - C to D: 7 * 0 = 0
   - D to A: (-2) * 7 = -14

   Sum these terms:
   
   \[
   S_1 = -54 + (-6) + 0 + (-14) = -74
   \]

4. **Compute the Second Sum (\( S_2 \)):**
   
   Calculate each term as \( y_i \times x_{i+1} \):

   - A to B: 7 * 2 = 14
   - B to C: 9 * 7 = 63
   - C to D: (-3) * (-2) = 6
   - D to A: 0 * (-6) = 0

   Sum these terms:
   
   \[
   S_2 = 14 + 63 + 6 + 0 = 83
   \]

5. **Calculate the Difference and Area:**
   
   Subtract \( S_2 \) from \( S_1 \):
   
   \[
   S_1 - S_2 = -74 - 83 = -157
   \]
   
   Take the absolute value:
   
   \[
   | -157 | = 157
   \]
   
   Divide by 2 to get the area:
   
   \[
   \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} \times 157 = 78.5
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The area of the quadrilateral is **78.5 square units**.

\boxed{78.5}

To find the equations of the perpendicular lines to the line connecting points (4, 4) and (-1, -1), we first determine the slope of the original line.

The slope \( m \) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{-1 - 4}{-1 - 4} = \frac{-5}{-5} = 1
\]

The slope of a line perpendicular to this is the negative reciprocal, which is \( m_{\perp} = -1 \).

Now, using the point-slope form for each given point:

For (4, 4):
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \\
y - 4 = -1(x - 4) \\
y = -x + 8
\]

For (-1, -1):
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \\
y - (-1) = -1(x - (-1)) \\
y + 1 = -x - 1 \\
y = -x - 2
\]

So, the equations of the perpendicular lines are:
- For (4, 4): \( y = -x + 8 \)
- For (-1, -1): \( y = -x - 2 \)

To find the slopes of two lines where one slope is twice the other and the tangent of the angle between them is 1/3:

- Let the slopes be \( m \) and \( 2m \).
- Use the formula for the tangent of the angle: 
  \[
  \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{2m - m}{1 + (m)(2m)} \right| = \left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right|
  \]
- Set this equal to \( \frac{1}{3} \):
  \[
  \left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right| = \frac{1}{3}
  \]
- Solving gives possible slopes of \( m = \pm 1 \) and \( m = \pm \frac{1}{2} \).

The final answers are:
- When \( m = 1 \): Slopes are 1 and 2.
- When \( m = -1 \): Slopes are -1 and -2.
- When \( m = \frac{1}{2} \): Slopes are \( \frac{1}{2} \) and 1.
- When \( m = -\frac{1}{2} \): Slopes are \( -\frac{1}{2} \) and -1.

Thus, the slopes can be represented as pairs: (1, 2), (-1, -2), (\(\frac{1}{2}\), 1), or (-\(\frac{1}{2}\), -1).

Since the line passes through (0, 5), substituting these coordinates into the equation gives k = 5. Therefore, the equation of the line parallel to the x-axis is y = 5.

Next, for a line to be parallel to the y-axis, its slope is undefined, and it takes the form x = k. Using the point (0, 5), we substitute these values into the equation to find that k = 0. Thus, the equation of the line parallel to the y-axis is x = 0.

You are an expert in solving problems related to the distance from a point to a line. However, your current request pertains to finding the **equations of lines** that are **parallel to the axes and pass through a specific point**, rather than calculating distances. Let me address this accordingly.

---

**Solution:**

We need to find the equations of two lines:
1. A line parallel to the x-axis.
2. A line parallel to the y-axis.

Both lines must pass through the given point \((0, 5)\).

### Step 1: Line Parallel to the X-Axis
A line that is **parallel to the x-axis** has a constant \(y\)-value. The general form of such a line is:
\[ y = k \]
Since the line passes through \((0, 5)\), we substitute \(x = 0\) and \(y = 5\) into the equation:
\[ 5 = k \]
Thus, the equation of the line parallel to the x-axis is:
\[ y = 5 \]

### Step 2: Line Parallel to the Y-Axis
A line that is **parallel to the y-axis** has a constant \(x\)-value. The general form of such a line is:
\[ x = k \]
Since the line passes through \((0, 5)\), we substitute \(y = 5\) into the equation (the value of \(k\) for this line depends only on the point where it crosses the y-axis):
\[ x = 0 \]

### Final Answer
The equations of the lines are:
- Parallel to the x-axis: \(\boxed{y = 5}\)
- Parallel to the y-axis: \(\boxed{x = 0}\)

Alright, let's tackle this problem step by step. We need to find the equation of the line that passes through the points (3, 1) and (5, 7). 

First, I'll recall that there are a few common forms of linear equations:
- **Slope-Intercept Form**: \( y = mx + b \), where \( m \) is the slope and \( b \) is the y-intercept.
- **Point-Slope Form**: \( y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \), which uses a point \((x_1, y_1)\) and the slope.
- **Standard Form**: \( Ax + By = C \), where \( A \), \( B \), and \( C \) are constants.

Since we have two points, the most straightforward way is to first find the slope (\( m \)) of the line using these points. The formula for the slope between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \]

Plugging in our points (3, 1) as \((x_1, y_1)\) and (5, 7) as \((x_2, y_2)\):
\[ m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3 \]

Now that we have the slope, we can use either Point-Slope or Slope-Intercept form to find the equation of the line. Let's choose **Slope-Intercept Form** for this explanation.

We'll start with:
\[ y = mx + b \]
We know \( m = 3 \), so:
\[ y = 3x + b \]

To find \( b \) (the y-intercept), we can plug in one of the given points. Let's use (3, 1):
\[ 1 = 3(3) + b \]
\[ 1 = 9 + b \]
Solving for \( b \):
\[ b = 1 - 9 \]
\[ b = -8 \]

Now we have all the components to write the equation of the line:
\[ y = 3x - 8 \]

So, the equation of the line that passes through (3, 1) and (5, 7) is \( y = 3x - 8 \).
</think>

Given that \(\tan \theta = \frac{1}{6}\), we need to find the equations.

First, we recognize that the tangent of an angle in a right triangle is equal to the slope of a line making that angle with the positive direction of the x-axis. Therefore, if \(\tan \theta = \frac{1}{6}\), the slope \(m\) of the line is also \(\frac{1}{6}\).

Since the problem does not provide additional information such as a specific point through which the line passes or an intercept, we cannot determine a unique equation. However, typically in such cases without more information, we can assume the line might pass through the origin. 

Thus, assuming the line passes through the origin, the equation of the line with slope \(\frac{1}{6}\) is:

\[
y = \frac{1}{6}x
\]

Therefore, the final answer is \(\boxed{y = \dfrac{1}{6}x}\).

To determine if the points \((3, 0)\), \((-2, -2)\), and \((8, 2)\) are collinear (lie on the same straight line), we can compare the slopes between pairs of these points.

1. **Calculate the slope between \((3, 0)\) and \((-2, -2)\):**
   
   \[
   \text{Slope} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{-2 - 0}{-2 - 3} = \frac{-2}{-5} = \frac{2}{5}
   \]

2. **Calculate the slope between \((-2, -2)\) and \((8, 2)\):**
   
   \[
   \text{Slope} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{2 - (-2)}{8 - (-2)} = \frac{4}{10} = \frac{2}{5}
   \]

Since both slopes are equal (\(\frac{2}{5}\)), the three points lie on the same straight line.

The formula for the distance \( d \) from a point \( (x_0, y_0) \) to a line \( ax + by + c = 0 \) is:

\[
d = \frac{|a x_0 + b y_0 + c|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

Given the line equation:
\[ 4x + 7y - 12 = 0 \]
Here, \( a = 4 \), \( b = 7 \), and \( c = -12 \).

The point is \( R(e, k) \). Plugging into the formula:

\[
d = \frac{|4e + 7k - 12|}{\sqrt{4^2 + 7^2}} = \frac{|4e + 7k - 12|}{\sqrt{65}}
\]

**Final Answer**
The distance from point R(e, k) to the line is given by the formula:

```json
{
  "Final Answer": {
    "Distance": f"\\boxed{{\\dfrac{{|4e + 7k - 12|}}{{\\sqrt{{65}}}}}}"
  }
}
```

The formula for the slope \( m \) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is:
\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \]

Plugging in the given values:
\[ m = \frac{3 - 0}{2 - 1} = \frac{3}{1} = 3 \]

The slope of any line perpendicular to this will be the negative reciprocal of \( m \). Therefore, the slope of the perpendicular line is:
\[ m_{\perp} = -\frac{1}{m} = -\frac{1}{3} \]

Now that I have the slope of the desired line, I can use the point-slope form to write its equation. Let's choose one of the given points; I'll pick (2, 3).

The point-slope form is:
\[ y - y_1 = m_{\perp}(x - x_1) \]

Substituting the values:
\[ y - 3 = -\frac{1}{3}(x - 2) \]

Simplifying this equation gives the final answer.

First, I need to find the equation of the line passing through the points (4, 7) and (-5, 8).

Once I have the slope, I can use the point-slope form of a linear equation to find the equation of the line.

Let's solve the problem step by step:

**Problem:**
Find the equation of the perpendicular bisector of the line segment joining the points \((4, 7)\) and \((-5, 8)\).

---

**Solution:**

1. **Find the Midpoint (\(M\)) of the Segment:**

   The midpoint \(M\) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
   \[
   M = \left(\frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2}\right)
   \]
   
   Substituting the given points:
   \[
   M_x = \frac{4 + (-5)}{2} = \frac{-1}{2}
   \]
   \[
   M_y = \frac{7 + 8}{2} = \frac{15}{2}
   \]
   
   So, the midpoint \(M\) is:
   \[
   M = \left( -\frac{1}{2}, \frac{15}{2} \right)
   \]

2. **Find the Slope (\(m\)) of the Segment:**

   The slope \(m\) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
   Substituting the given points:
   \[
   m = \frac{8 - 7}{-5 - 4} = \frac{1}{-9} = -\frac{1}{9}
   \]

3. **Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Bisector:**

   The slope of a line perpendicular to another with slope \(m\) is:
   \[
   m_{\perp} = -\frac{1}{m}
   \]
   
   Substituting \(m = -\frac{1}{9}\):
   \[
   m_{\perp} = -\frac{1}{-\frac{1}{9}} = 9
   \]

4. **Find the Equation of the Perpendicular Bisector:**

   Using the point-slope form of a line:
   \[
   y - M_y = m_{\perp}(x - M_x)
   \]
   
   Substituting \(M = \left( -\frac{1}{2}, \frac{15}{2} \right)\) and \(m_{\perp} = 9\):
   \[
   y - \frac{15}{2} = 9\left(x + \frac{1}{2}\right)
   \]
   
   Simplifying:
   \[
   y = 9x + \frac{9}{2} + \frac{15}{2}
   \]
   \[
   y = 9x + \frac{24}{2}
   \]
   \[
   y = 9x + 12
   \]

---

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\text{The equation of the perpendicular bisector is } y = 9x + 12}

The equation of the perpendicular bisector is found by calculating the midpoint and using the negative reciprocal slope of the original line segment between points (1,4) and (5,6).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Find the Midpoint:**  
   The midpoint \( M \) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is given by:
   \[
   M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
   \]
   Plugging in the points (1,4) and (5,6):
   \[
   M = \left( \frac{1 + 5}{2}, \frac{4 + 6}{2} \right) = (3, 5)
   \]

2. **Calculate the Slope of the Original Line:**  
   The slope \( m \) between two points is:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   Using the given points:
   \[
   m = \frac{6 - 4}{5 - 1} = \frac{2}{4} = \frac{1}{2}
   \]

3. **Determine the Perpendicular Slope:**  
   The slope of a perpendicular line is the negative reciprocal of the original slope:
   \[
   m_{\text{perp}} = -\frac{1}{m} = -\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}} = -2
   \]

4. **Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation:**  
   The point-slope form of a line is:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   Using the midpoint \( (3, 5) \) and the perpendicular slope \( -2 \):
   \[
   y - 5 = -2(x - 3)
   \]

5. **Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form:**  
   Expanding the equation:
   \[
   y - 5 = -2x + 6
   \]
   Adding 5 to both sides:
   \[
   y = -2x + 11
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the perpendicular bisector is \( y = -2x + 11 \).

```json
{"Final Answer": "y = -2x + 11"}
```

To find the distance from a point to the line given by \( \frac{x}{3} + \frac{y}{4} = 1 \), we first rewrite it in standard form:

\[ 4x + 3y - 12 = 0 \]

Using the distance formula, if considering the origin (0,0) as the point, the distance \( d \) is calculated as follows:

\[
d = \frac{|4(0) + 3(0) - 12|}{\sqrt{4^2 + 3^2}} = \frac{12}{5}
\]

Thus, the final answer is:
\[

\[10y = 24x + 328\]
\[y = \frac{24}{10}x + \frac{328}{10}\]
\[y = 2.4x + 32.8\]

\[
d = \frac{|Ax_0 + By_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

\[ -2.4x + y - 32.8 = 0 \]

\[

To find the distance from the point \((-2, 2)\) to the line defined by \(24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Simplify the Given Equation
First, simplify the equation into slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)).

\[
24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)
\]
\[
24x + 288 = 10y - 40
\]
\[
10y = 24x + 328 \quad \text{(Adding 40 to both sides)}
\]
\[
y = \frac{24}{10}x + \frac{328}{10}
\]
\[
y = 2.4x + 32.8
\]

### Step 2: Use the Distance Formula from a Point to a Line
The formula to find the distance (\(d\)) from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\) is:

\[
d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Rewrite the equation in standard form (\(Ax + By + C = 0\)):

\[
y - 2.4x - 32.8 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad -2.4x + y - 32.8 = 0
\]
So, \(A = -2.4\), \(B = 1\), and \(C = -32.8\).

### Step 3: Plug in the Values
Using the point \((-2, 2)\):

\[
d = \frac{|-2.4(-2) + 1(2) - 32.8|}{\sqrt{(-2.4)^2 + (1)^2}} = \frac{|4.8 + 2 - 32.8|}{\sqrt{5.76 + 1}} = \frac{|-26|}{\sqrt{6.76}} = \frac{26}{2.6} = 10
\]

### Final Answer

```json
{"Final Answer": "The distance is 10 units."}
```

- Forward unit vector: \(\left( \frac{7}{\sqrt{65}}, -\frac{4}{\sqrt{65}} \right)\)
- Backward unit vector: \(\left( -\frac{7}{\sqrt{65}}, \frac{4}{\sqrt{65}} \right)\)

Point A: \( \left( 1 + \frac{7\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{65}}, 2 - \frac{4\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{65}} \right) \)

To find the distance between two parallel lines given by the equations \(3x + 4y = 9\) and \(6x + 8y = 18\), I first need to ensure that both equations are in the standard form.

I notice that the second equation can be simplified by dividing all terms by 2, which gives me \(3x + 4y = 9\). Now, both equations are identical:

To find the **distance** between two parallel lines given by the equations:

\[3x + 4y = 9\]
\[6x + 8y = 18\]

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Simplify the Second Equation:**
   
   Notice that the second equation can be simplified:
   \[
   6x + 8y = 18 \implies \frac{6}{2}x + \frac{8}{2}y = \frac{18}{2} \implies 3x + 4y = 9
   \]
   
   Now, both equations are identical:
   \[
   3x + 4y = 9
   \]

2. **Conclusion:**
   
   Since both equations represent the **same line**, there is no distinct distance between them. They completely overlap.

### Final Answer:

\[
\boxed{0}
\]

The distance from the point (4,1) to the line 4x - y = 0 is calculated using the formula for the distance from a point to a line.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Line Equation and Point:**
   - The given line equation is \(4x - y = 0\).
   - The point is (4, 1).

2. **Rewrite the Line Equation in Standard Form:**
   - The standard form of a line is \(Ax + By + C = 0\).
   - Here, we can rewrite the given equation as \(4x - y + 0 = 0\), so A=4, B=-1, and C=0.

3. **Apply the Distance Formula:**
   - The distance \(D\) from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\) is given by:
     \[
     D = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
     \]
   - Plugging in the values: \(x_0=4\), \(y_0=1\), A=4, B=-1, C=0.
   
4. **Calculate Numerator and Denominator:**
   - Numerator: \(|4*4 + (-1)*1 + 0| = |16 -1| = 15\).
   - Denominator: \(\sqrt{4^2 + (-1)^2} = \sqrt{16 + 1} = \sqrt{17}\).

5. **Compute the Distance:**
   - \(D = \frac{15}{\sqrt{17}}\). This can also be written as \(\frac{15\sqrt{17}}{17}\) after rationalizing.

**Final Answer:**

```json
{"Final Answer": "15/√17"}
```

The required line is equidistant from both given lines and parallel to them. By averaging the constants of the original lines after adjusting their coefficients, we find the equation of the desired line.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Ensure Parallelism:**
   - Both lines must have the same coefficients for x and y.
   - Original Line: \(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\)
   - Given Line: Multiply by 3 to get \(9x + 6y + 18 = 0\)

2. **Calculate Mid-Constant (c):**
   - Average the constants of both lines:
     \[
     c_{\text{mid}} = \frac{-7 + 18}{2} = \frac{11}{2}
     \]

3. **Form the New Line Equation:**
   - Using the same coefficients and mid-constant:
     \[
     9x + 6y + \frac{11}{2} = 0
     \]
   - Multiply through by 2 to eliminate fractions:
     \[
     18x + 12y + 11 = 0
     \]
